{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01B6T13TVxGcGKNLxcRvueV2","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→# Indian Health Service — Product Research\n     2→\n     3→Researched: 2026-02-15\n     4→Developer website: https://www.ihs.gov/promotinginteroperability/certificationoverview/\n     5→\n     6→## Overview\n     7→\n     8→The Indian Health Service (IHS) is a federal agency within the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services responsible for providing healthcare to approximately 2.6 million members of 574 federally recognized American Indian and Alaska Native (AI/AN) tribes. IHS is not a commercial vendor — it is a government healthcare provider and developer that built its own health information system beginning in the early 1980s. The agency operates 45 hospitals and over 600 other facilities (clinics, health stations, etc.) across 12 service areas, primarily in rural and remote locations.\n     9→\n    10→IHS developed the Resource and Patient Management System (RPMS) as a comprehensive, decentralized health information system. RPMS is built on the same technology foundation as the VA's VistA system — both descend from the VA's Decentralized Hospital Computer Program (DHCP) and run on MUMPS (M) programming language atop InterSystems Caché/IRIS databases. RPMS was first deployed around 1984 and has been continuously developed and maintained by IHS since then.\n    11→\n    12→The system serves three user communities (the \"I/T/U\" community): IHS-operated federal facilities, tribal health programs operating under self-governance compacts, and urban Indian health organizations. Some tribal programs have transitioned to commercial EHRs (Cerner, NextGen, athenahealth), and in November 2023, IHS selected General Dynamics Information Technology (GDIT) to implement a new enterprise EHR using Oracle Cerner technology — a potential $2.5 billion, 10-year contract. As of 2025-2026, RPMS remains operational and certified while the modernization effort proceeds.\n    13→\n    14→## Product: Resource and Patient Management System Electronic Health Record\n    15→\n    16→CHPL IDs: 11717\n    17→\n    18→### What It Is\n    19→\n    20→RPMS EHR is a comprehensive, integrated health information system covering clinical, administrative, and financial functions. It is not a single application but a suite of approximately 50-100 software packages running on a shared MUMPS/FileMan database. The certified module (version BCERv9.0, certified 2025-11-24) encompasses the full breadth of RPMS functionality — clinical documentation, pharmacy, laboratory, radiology, scheduling, billing, immunizations, public health reporting, and more.\n    21→\n    22→The EHR graphical interface is built on the VueCentric framework (owned by Medsphere Systems Corporation), which provides a Windows-based GUI client that communicates with the RPMS server via Remote Procedure Call (RPC) Broker. The underlying database layer uses VA FileMan for data management, with VA Kernel providing the runtime environment.\n    23→\n    24→RPMS was the first VistA-related EHR certified for Meaningful Use Stage I and holds broad ONC certification across 42 criteria covering clinical data (a)(1)-(a)(14), transitions of care (b)(1)-(b)(3), clinical quality measures (c)(1)-(c)(3), security (d)(1)-(d)(13), patient access (e)(1), public health reporting (f)(1)-(f)(6), APIs including FHIR (g)(7)-(g)(10), and immunization registry exchange (h)(1).\n    25→\n    26→### Users & Market\n    27→\n    28→**Primary users** (per the SED description): medical providers, nursing staff, health information management staff, pharmacy staff, and imaging and laboratory personnel at clinics and hospitals.\n    29→\n    30→**Scale**: IHS serves 2.6 million AI/AN patients across 574 tribes. As of the most recent assessments, hundreds of IHS and tribal sites run RPMS. A 1993 GAO report documented 229 operational sites; deployment expanded significantly through the 2000s. The system is also used at some Hawaii public health sites and select American Guam clinics.\n    31→\n    32→**Clinical settings**: The system supports both ambulatory clinics and hospitals, including emergency departments, inpatient wards, dental clinics, behavioral health, optometry, and public health programs. IHS facilities tend to be in rural and remote areas, often serving as the sole healthcare provider for their communities.\n    33→\n    34→**User satisfaction is low**: A Regenstrief Institute assessment found 60.3% of users believe RPMS needs \"significant improvements or a complete overhaul,\" 30.3% rated overall quality as \"poor\" or \"very poor,\" and 93% agreed it was time to replace the system. The planned Oracle Cerner transition reflects this assessment.\n    35→\n    36→**Notable departures**: Alaska Native Medical Center switched to Cerner in the mid-2000s. Multiple California tribal sites have adopted NextGen. Community Health Clinics of West Virginia used RPMS via Medsphere but later transitioned to athenahealth.\n    37→\n    38→### Modules & Functionality\n    39→\n    40→RPMS comprises a large suite of packages organized into clinical, administrative, and infrastructure categories. The following is based on the IHS RPMS applications pages and historical documentation.\n    41→\n    42→**Core Clinical Applications:**\n    43→- **Patient Care Component (PCC)**: The clinical heart of RPMS — a centralized clinical data repository using a \"V-file\" architecture (V MEASUREMENT, V PROVIDER, V POV, V LAB, V MICROBIOLOGY, V BLOOD BANK, V HOSPITALIZATION, etc.) where all visit-related data links to a central VISIT file. PCC was revolutionary when designed in 1983-85 and its fundamental structure has remained stable since. It enables population health queries and integrated billing through its relational visit-file design.\n    44→- **Electronic Health Record GUI (EHR)**: Graphical user interface built on VueCentric framework providing the clinician-facing application.\n    45→- **Text Integration Utility (TIU)**: Template-based clinical note authoring for clinic notes and other documentation.\n    46→- **Outpatient Pharmacy (PSO)**: Management and reporting of outpatient medication regimens.\n    47→- **IHS Pharmacy MOD (APSP)**: IHS-specific modifications to VA pharmacy packages.\n    48→- **Electronic Prescribing of Controlled Substances (BEPC)**: DEA-compliant e-prescribing for controlled substances.\n    49→- **e-Prescribing Productions (BEPR)**: Ensemble interface engine for pharmacy network integration and Surescripts connectivity.\n    50→- **Pharmacy - National Drug File (PSN)**: Drug data management from FDA sources.\n    51→- **Pharmacy - Controlled Drug Export System (BPDM)**: Export files for controlled substance dispensing.\n    52→- **Laboratory (LR)**: Comprehensive laboratory/pathology data and record keeping across all pathology areas.\n    53→- **Electronic Laboratory Reporting (BLE)**: HL7 message generation for lab result reporting (including COVID-19).\n    54→- **Adverse Reaction Tracking (GMRA)**: Allergy and adverse reaction documentation.\n    55→- **Immunization Tracking System (BI)**: Immunization data compilation, reporting, and adverse reaction tracking — described as \"the crown jewel of RPMS.\"\n    56→- **Immunization Interface Management (BYIM)**: Bidirectional HL7 interface with state immunization registries.\n    57→- **Consult/Request Tracking (GMRC)**: Consultation and referral management.\n    58→- **Dental Data System (ADE)**: Dental care data capture.\n    59→- **Dental/EDR Interface (BADE)**: Bidirectional data exchange with Electronic Dental Record systems.\n    60→- **Behavioral Health System (AMH)**: Encounters, group services, treatment plans, case management for behavioral health.\n    61→- **Women's Health (WH)**: Women's health tracking and reporting.\n    62→- **Diabetes Management System (BDM)**: Specialized diabetic patient tracking and treatment assessment.\n    63→- **HIV Management System (BKM)**: HIV case management.\n    64→- **Emergency Room System (AMER)**: ER registration, admission, discharge, and management reporting.\n    65→- **Emergency Department Dashboard (BEDD)**: Emergency/urgent care operations management.\n    66→- **Chronic Disease Management (BCDM)**: Chronic condition management.\n    67→- **Care Management Event Tracking (CMET/BTPW)**: Routine patient event follow-up for care management.\n    68→- **Case Management System (ACM)**: Patient registers for managing select patient groupings.\n    69→- **Community Health Representative System (BCH)**: Documentation of community health activities.\n    70→- **Prenatal Module**: Prenatal care documentation.\n    71→- **Well-Child Module**: Questionnaire-oriented pediatric well-child visit forms.\n    72→- **Optometry**: Structured optometry reading documentation.\n    73→\n    74→**Administrative and Financial Applications:**\n    75→- **Patient Registration (AG)**: Patient demographic and insurance eligibility database.\n    76→- **Patient Information Management System (PIMS)**: ADT (Admission/Discharge/Transfer), clinic scheduling, and sensitive patient tracking.\n    77→- **Third Party Billing (ABM)**: Insurance claims generation for Medicare, Medicaid, and private insurance (CMS-1500, UB-04, ASC X12 837).\n    78→- **Accounts Receivable (BAR)**: Third-party billing transaction processing and follow-up.\n    79→- **Pharmacy Point of Sale (ABSP)**: Online pharmacy claims submission to third-party payers.\n    80→- **Contract Health Services/Management Info System (ACHS)**: Document and fiscal management for outside provider referrals (now called Purchased/Referred Care).\n    81→- **Administrative Resource Management System (ACR)**: Requisitions and purchase orders.\n    82→- **Practice Management Application Suite (BPRM)**: Browser-based GUI for business functions.\n    83→\n    84→**Population Health and Quality Reporting:**\n    85→- **Clinical Reporting System (BGP)**: GPRA (Government Performance and Results Act) and clinical performance measure data production.\n    86→- **iCare Population Management GUI (BQI)**: Windows-based tool for population health data retrieval.\n    87→- **eCQM Engine and Export Tool**: Clinical quality measure calculation and QRDA CAT-I export for CMS.\n    88→- **Computerized Public Health Activity Data System (BNI)**: Public health activities documentation.\n    89→- **Data Warehouse Export System (BDW)**: Export to IHS National Data Warehouse.\n    90→\n    91→**Interoperability:**\n    92→- **Consolidated Clinical Document Architecture (BCCD)**: C-CDA Release 2.1 document generation.\n    93→- **C32/CCD Clinical Summary (BJMD)**: HITSP-format clinical summary generation.\n    94→- **Generic Interface System (GIS)**: HL7-based demographic and PCC data exchange.\n    95→- **IHS Support for HL7 Interfaces (BHL)**: HL7 data exchange with commercial and other applications.\n    96→- **Personal Health Record (BPHR)**: Patient Health Record and Direct Secure Messaging.\n    97→- **Master Patient Index (AGMP)**: Cross-facility patient identity management.\n    98→- **RPMS EHI Export (BREH)**: ONC-compliant EHI export using predefined national schema.\n    99→\n   100→**Infrastructure:**\n   101→- VA FileMan (database management), VA Kernel (runtime), MailMan (internal messaging), RPC Broker (client-server communication), BMXNet (.NET framework utilities), IHS Code Mapping (SNOMED/LOINC mapping), and various toolkit extensions.\n   102→\n   103→### Data & Content\n   104→\n   105→Based on the module inventory and PCC architecture, RPMS stores an exceptionally broad range of health data:\n   106→\n   107→**Clinical data**: Patient demographics and insurance eligibility, visit/encounter records (via PCC V-file structure), clinical notes (via TIU templates), problem lists with coded diagnoses, medication records (outpatient pharmacy, controlled substances, e-prescribing), allergies and adverse reactions, laboratory results across all pathology areas, radiology orders and results, immunization records including adverse reactions, vital signs and measurements, dental care records, behavioral health encounters and treatment plans, women's health data, diabetes management data, HIV case management, prenatal care, well-child visit data, optometry readings, surgical/day surgery data, emergency department records, and consult/referral tracking.\n   108→\n   109→**Administrative and financial data**: ADT records, scheduling/appointments, insurance claims (Medicare, Medicaid, private — CMS-1500, UB-04, X12 837 formats), accounts receivable and third-party billing transactions, pharmacy point-of-sale claims, contract/purchased referred care documentation, requisitions and purchase orders.\n   110→\n   111→**Population health and reporting data**: GPRA performance measures, eCQM quality measures (QRDA CAT-I), community health representative activity data, epidemiological data accumulated over decades, public health activity data, national data warehouse exports.\n   112→\n   113→**Interoperability artifacts**: C-CDA documents, HL7 messages (demographic and clinical), immunization registry exchange messages, FHIR API data (per g(10) certification), Direct Secure Messaging, EHI export files.\n   114→\n   115→**Internal system data**: MailMan messages (internal electronic mail), user security audit logs, site tracking data, code mapping tables (SNOMED, LOINC, ICD, CPT).\n   116→\n   117→The PCC V-file architecture is the central repository linking all clinical data to visits. This design — unchanged since the mid-1980s — is what enables RPMS's population health query capabilities and integrated billing. The EHI Export package (BREH) generates exports using a nationally defined schema published at the IHS FTP site, with schema definition documents and versioned schema text files (2023, 2024, 2025).\n   118→\n   119→**Notable**: RPMS has decades of accumulated population health data for AI/AN communities. A key concern in the Oracle Cerner transition is that only 24 months of recent clinical data will migrate — the rest will be archived in a \"Four Directions Warehouse\" with API-based access.\n   120→\n   121→---\n   122→\n   123→## Relationship to VistA\n   124→\n   125→RPMS and VistA share the same foundational infrastructure (Kernel, FileMan, MailMan, drug files, code sets) but diverged significantly in clinical data architecture. RPMS was originally focused on outpatient/ambulatory care for diverse AI/AN populations; VistA was focused on inpatient/hospital care for veterans. The most significant divergence is between PCC (RPMS's clinical repository) and PCE (Patient Care Encounter — the VA's adaptation of PCC, which was heavily rewritten). Many RPMS packages originated as VistA imports (Pharmacy, Lab, Radiology, Scheduling) but have been extensively modified. Conversely, the VA adopted RPMS innovations including the Problem List and PCC concept. A convergence analysis found varying difficulty levels: some packages differ in only a few routines, while Lab has 819 of 1,132 routines modified plus 197 IHS extensions, and PCC vs PCE represents a fundamentally different clinical philosophy.\n   126→\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. IHS is not a commercial vendor — it is a government healthcare provider and developer that built its own health information system beginning in the early 1980s. The agency operates 45 hospitals and over 600 other facilities (clinics, health stations, etc.) across 12 service areas, primarily in rural and remote locations.\n\nIHS developed the Resource and Patient Management System (RPMS) as a comprehensive, decentralized health information system. RPMS is built on the same technology foundation as the VA's VistA system — both descend from the VA's Decentralized Hospital Computer Program (DHCP) and run on MUMPS (M) programming language atop InterSystems Caché/IRIS databases. RPMS was first deployed around 1984 and has been continuously developed and maintained by IHS since then.\n\nThe system serves three user communities (the \"I/T/U\" community): IHS-operated federal facilities, tribal health programs operating under self-governance compacts, and urban Indian health organizations. Some tribal programs have transitioned to commercial EHRs (Cerner, NextGen, athenahealth), and in November 2023, IHS selected General Dynamics Information Technology (GDIT) to implement a new enterprise EHR using Oracle Cerner technology — a potential $2.5 billion, 10-year contract. As of 2025-2026, RPMS remains operational and certified while the modernization effort proceeds.\n\n## Product: Resource and Patient Management System Electronic Health Record\n\nCHPL IDs: 11717\n\n### What It Is\n\nRPMS EHR is a comprehensive, integrated health information system covering clinical, administrative, and financial functions. It is not a single application but a suite of approximately 50-100 software packages running on a shared MUMPS/FileMan database. The certified module (version BCERv9.0, certified 2025-11-24) encompasses the full breadth of RPMS functionality — clinical documentation, pharmacy, laboratory, radiology, scheduling, billing, immunizations, public health reporting, and more.\n\nThe EHR graphical interface is built on the VueCentric framework (owned by Medsphere Systems Corporation), which provides a Windows-based GUI client that communicates with the RPMS server via Remote Procedure Call (RPC) Broker. The underlying database layer uses VA FileMan for data management, with VA Kernel providing the runtime environment.\n\nRPMS was the first VistA-related EHR certified for Meaningful Use Stage I and holds broad ONC certification across 42 criteria covering clinical data (a)(1)-(a)(14), transitions of care (b)(1)-(b)(3), clinical quality measures (c)(1)-(c)(3), security (d)(1)-(d)(13), patient access (e)(1), public health reporting (f)(1)-(f)(6), APIs including FHIR (g)(7)-(g)(10), and immunization registry exchange (h)(1).\n\n### Users & Market\n\n**Primary users** (per the SED description): medical providers, nursing staff, health information management staff, pharmacy staff, and imaging and laboratory personnel at clinics and hospitals.\n\n**Scale**: IHS serves 2.6 million AI/AN patients across 574 tribes. As of the most recent assessments, hundreds of IHS and tribal sites run RPMS. A 1993 GAO report documented 229 operational sites; deployment expanded significantly through the 2000s. The system is also used at some Hawaii public health sites and select American Guam clinics.\n\n**Clinical settings**: The system supports both ambulatory clinics and hospitals, including emergency departments, inpatient wards, dental clinics, behavioral health, optometry, and public health programs. IHS facilities tend to be in rural and remote areas, often serving as the sole healthcare provider for their communities.\n\n**User satisfaction is low**: A Regenstrief Institute assessment found 60.3% of users believe RPMS needs \"significant improvements or a complete overhaul,\" 30.3% rated overall quality as \"poor\" or \"very poor,\" and 93% agreed it was time to replace the system. The planned Oracle Cerner transition reflects this assessment.\n\n**Notable departures**: Alaska Native Medical Center switched to Cerner in the mid-2000s. Multiple California tribal sites have adopted NextGen. Community Health Clinics of West Virginia used RPMS via Medsphere but later transitioned to athenahealth.\n\n### Modules & Functionality\n\nRPMS comprises a large suite of packages organized into clinical, administrative, and infrastructure categories. The following is based on the IHS RPMS applications pages and historical documentation.\n\n**Core Clinical Applications:**\n- **Patient Care Component (PCC)**: The clinical heart of RPMS — a centralized clinical data repository using a \"V-file\" architecture (V MEASUREMENT, V PROVIDER, V POV, V LAB, V MICROBIOLOGY, V BLOOD BANK, V HOSPITALIZATION, etc.) where all visit-related data links to a central VISIT file. PCC was revolutionary when designed in 1983-85 and its fundamental structure has remained stable since. It enables population health queries and integrated billing through its relational visit-file design.\n- **Electronic Health Record GUI (EHR)**: Graphical user interface built on VueCentric framework providing the clinician-facing application.\n- **Text Integration Utility (TIU)**: Template-based clinical note authoring for clinic notes and other documentation.\n- **Outpatient Pharmacy (PSO)**: Management and reporting of outpatient medication regimens.\n- **IHS Pharmacy MOD (APSP)**: IHS-specific modifications to VA pharmacy packages.\n- **Electronic Prescribing of Controlled Substances (BEPC)**: DEA-compliant e-prescribing for controlled substances.\n- **e-Prescribing Productions (BEPR)**: Ensemble interface engine for pharmacy network integration and Surescripts connectivity.\n- **Pharmacy - National Drug File (PSN)**: Drug data management from FDA sources.\n- **Pharmacy - Controlled Drug Export System (BPDM)**: Export files for controlled substance dispensing.\n- **Laboratory (LR)**: Comprehensive laboratory/pathology data and record keeping across all pathology areas.\n- **Electronic Laboratory Reporting (BLE)**: HL7 message generation for lab result reporting (including COVID-19).\n- **Adverse Reaction Tracking (GMRA)**: Allergy and adverse reaction documentation.\n- **Immunization Tracking System (BI)**: Immunization data compilation, reporting, and adverse reaction tracking — described as \"the crown jewel of RPMS.\"\n- **Immunization Interface Management (BYIM)**: Bidirectional HL7 interface with state immunization registries.\n- **Consult/Request Tracking (GMRC)**: Consultation and referral management.\n- **Dental Data System (ADE)**: Dental care data capture.\n- **Dental/EDR Interface (BADE)**: Bidirectional data exchange with Electronic Dental Record systems.\n- **Behavioral Health System (AMH)**: Encounters, group services, treatment plans, case management for behavioral health.\n- **Women's Health (WH)**: Women's health tracking and reporting.\n- **Diabetes Management System (BDM)**: Specialized diabetic patient tracking and treatment assessment.\n- **HIV Management System (BKM)**: HIV case management.\n- **Emergency Room System (AMER)**: ER registration, admission, discharge, and management reporting.\n- **Emergency Department Dashboard (BEDD)**: Emergency/urgent care operations management.\n- **Chronic Disease Management (BCDM)**: Chronic condition management.\n- **Care Management Event Tracking (CMET/BTPW)**: Routine patient event follow-up for care management.\n- **Case Management System (ACM)**: Patient registers for managing select patient groupings.\n- **Community Health Representative System (BCH)**: Documentation of community health activities.\n- **Prenatal Module**: Prenatal care documentation.\n- **Well-Child Module**: Questionnaire-oriented pediatric well-child visit forms.\n- **Optometry**: Structured optometry reading documentation.\n\n**Administrative and Financial Applications:**\n- **Patient Registration (AG)**: Patient demographic and insurance eligibility database.\n- **Patient Information Management System (PIMS)**: ADT (Admission/Discharge/Transfer), clinic scheduling, and sensitive patient tracking.\n- **Third Party Billing (ABM)**: Insurance claims generation for Medicare, Medicaid, and private insurance (CMS-1500, UB-04, ASC X12 837).\n- **Accounts Receivable (BAR)**: Third-party billing transaction processing and follow-up.\n- **Pharmacy Point of Sale (ABSP)**: Online pharmacy claims submission to third-party payers.\n- **Contract Health Services/Management Info System (ACHS)**: Document and fiscal management for outside provider referrals (now called Purchased/Referred Care).\n- **Administrative Resource Management System (ACR)**: Requisitions and purchase orders.\n- **Practice Management Application Suite (BPRM)**: Browser-based GUI for business functions.\n\n**Population Health and Quality Reporting:**\n- **Clinical Reporting System (BGP)**: GPRA (Government Performance and Results Act) and clinical performance measure data production.\n- **iCare Population Management GUI (BQI)**: Windows-based tool for population health data retrieval.\n- **eCQM Engine and Export Tool**: Clinical quality measure calculation and QRDA CAT-I export for CMS.\n- **Computerized Public Health Activity Data System (BNI)**: Public health activities documentation.\n- **Data Warehouse Export System (BDW)**: Export to IHS National Data Warehouse.\n\n**Interoperability:**\n- **Consolidated Clinical Document Architecture (BCCD)**: C-CDA Release 2.1 document generation.\n- **C32/CCD Clinical Summary (BJMD)**: HITSP-format clinical summary generation.\n- **Generic Interface System (GIS)**: HL7-based demographic and PCC data exchange.\n- **IHS Support for HL7 Interfaces (BHL)**: HL7 data exchange with commercial and other applications.\n- **Personal Health Record (BPHR)**: Patient Health Record and Direct Secure Messaging.\n- **Master Patient Index (AGMP)**: Cross-facility patient identity management.\n- **RPMS EHI Export (BREH)**: ONC-compliant EHI export using predefined national schema.\n\n**Infrastructure:**\n- VA FileMan (database management), VA Kernel (runtime), MailMan (internal messaging), RPC Broker (client-server communication), BMXNet (.NET framework utilities), IHS Code Mapping (SNOMED/LOINC mapping), and various toolkit extensions.\n\n### Data & Content\n\nBased on the module inventory and PCC architecture, RPMS stores an exceptionally broad range of health data:\n\n**Clinical data**: Patient demographics and insurance eligibility, visit/encounter records (via PCC V-file structure), clinical notes (via TIU templates), problem lists with coded diagnoses, medication records (outpatient pharmacy, controlled substances, e-prescribing), allergies and adverse reactions, laboratory results across all pathology areas, radiology orders and results, immunization records including adverse reactions, vital signs and measurements, dental care records, behavioral health encounters and treatment plans, women's health data, diabetes management data, HIV case management, prenatal care, well-child visit data, optometry readings, surgical/day surgery data, emergency department records, and consult/referral tracking.\n\n**Administrative and financial data**: ADT records, scheduling/appointments, insurance claims (Medicare, Medicaid, private — CMS-1500, UB-04, X12 837 formats), accounts receivable and third-party billing transactions, pharmacy point-of-sale claims, contract/purchased referred care documentation, requisitions and purchase orders.\n\n**Population health and reporting data**: GPRA performance measures, eCQM quality measures (QRDA CAT-I), community health representative activity data, epidemiological data accumulated over decades, public health activity data, national data warehouse exports.\n\n**Interoperability artifacts**: C-CDA documents, HL7 messages (demographic and clinical), immunization registry exchange messages, FHIR API data (per g(10) certification), Direct Secure Messaging, EHI export files.\n\n**Internal system data**: MailMan messages (internal electronic mail), user security audit logs, site tracking data, code mapping tables (SNOMED, LOINC, ICD, CPT).\n\nThe PCC V-file architecture is the central repository linking all clinical data to visits. This design — unchanged since the mid-1980s — is what enables RPMS's population health query capabilities and integrated billing. The EHI Export package (BREH) generates exports using a nationally defined schema published at the IHS FTP site, with schema definition documents and versioned schema text files (2023, 2024, 2025).\n\n**Notable**: RPMS has decades of accumulated population health data for AI/AN communities. A key concern in the Oracle Cerner transition is that only 24 months of recent clinical data will migrate — the rest will be archived in a \"Four Directions Warehouse\" with API-based access.\n\n---\n\n## Relationship to VistA\n\nRPMS and VistA share the same foundational infrastructure (Kernel, FileMan, MailMan, drug files, code sets) but diverged significantly in clinical data architecture. RPMS was originally focused on outpatient/ambulatory care for diverse AI/AN populations; VistA was focused on inpatient/hospital care for veterans. The most significant divergence is between PCC (RPMS's clinical repository) and PCE (Patient Care Encounter — the VA's adaptation of PCC, which was heavily rewritten). Many RPMS packages originated as VistA imports (Pharmacy, Lab, Radiology, Scheduling) but have been extensively modified. Conversely, the VA adopted RPMS innovations including the Problem List and PCC concept. A convergence analysis found varying difficulty levels: some packages differ in only a few routines, while Lab has 819 of 1,132 routines modified plus 197 IHS extensions, and PCC vs PCE represents a fundamentally different clinical philosophy.\n","numLines":126,"startLine":1,"totalLines":126}}}

This predefined schema is published on the Indian Health Service\nwebsite.\nEHI exports contain the EHI available in the IHS certified RPMS Electronic Health\nRecord (EHR) in a JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) format for the patient.\nDownload the IHS EHI national schema from the IHS website for more information\nabout the data included in the EHI JSON export.\nNote: Although an EHI export generates using a predefined\nnational schema, the system provides sites using the IHS\ncertified EHR with an option to create their own schema.\nSite-defined schemas are based on the national schema.\nAll EHI information is available in an EHI export, with the exception of images.\nImaging links/data are presented but not the images themselves in accordance with\ncertification requirements.\nSeveral factors may determine the content available in the export, including:\n•\n\nThe version of the EHR system that generated the export file.\n\n•\n\nThe software packages installed on the EHR system that generated the export file.\n\n•\n\nThe version of the national schema that generated the export file.\n\n•\n\nConfiguration decisions made by the site that generated the file, including\ndocumentation, software workflows and use cases, use of a custom schema, etc.\n\nIHS EHI exports are best viewed using a JSON viewer or html compliant text\neditor/viewer.\nEHI means Electronic Protected Health Information (ePHI) as defined in 45 CFR\n160.103 to the extent that it would be included in a Designated Record Set (DRS) as\ndefined in 45 CFR 164.501, regardless of whether the group of records are used or\nmaintained by or for an entity covered by the Health Insurance Portability and\nAccountability Act (HIPAA) Rules.\n\nIHS EHI Schema Definition\nSeptember 2023\n\nElectronic Health Information Export\n\n1\n\n\fRPMS Electronic Health Information (EHI) Export (BREH)\n\nVersion 1.0\n\nThe DRS, as defined in the HIPAA Rules (45 CFR 164.501), consists of the\nfollowing:\n•\n\nMedical records and billing records about individuals.\n\n•\n\nEnrollment, payment, claims adjudication, and case or medical management\nrecord systems maintained by or for a health plan.\n\n•\n\nOther records that are used, in whole or in part to make decisions about\nindividuals.\nNote: The term record means any item, collection, or grouping of\ninformation that includes protected health information.\n\nAn EHI export does not include the following:\n•\n\nPsychotherapy notes as defined in 45CFR 164.501.\n\n•\n\nInformation compiled in reasonable anticipation of, or for use in, a civil, criminal,\nor administrative action or proceeding.\n\nIHS EHI Schema Definition\nSeptember 2023\n\nElectronic Health Information Export\n\n2\n\n\fRPMS Electronic Health Information (EHI) Export (BREH)\n\nVersion 1.0\n\nAcronym List\nAcronym\n\nTerm Meaning\n\nDRS\n\nDesignated Record Set\n\nEHI\n\nElectronic Health Information\n\nEHR\n\nElectronic Health manually\ngenerate an EHI export using a predefined national schema for a single patient or a\npatient population. This predefined schema is published on the Indian Health Service\nwebsite.\nEHI exports contain the EHI available in the IHS certified RPMS Electronic Health\nRecord (EHR) in a JavaScript Object schema.\nAll EHI information is available in an EHI export, with the exception of images.\nImaging links/data are presented but not the images themselves in accordance with\ncertification including\ndocumentation, software workflows and use cases, use of a custom schema, etc.\n\nIHS EHI exports are best viewed using a JSON viewer or html compliant text\neditor/viewer.\nEHI means Electronic Protected Health Information (ePHI) as defined in 45 CFR\n160.103 to the extent that it would be included in a Designated Record Set (DRS) as\ndefined in 45 CFR 164.501, regardless of whether the group of records are used or\nmaintained by or for an entity covered by the Health Insurance Portability and\nAccountability Act (HIPAA) Rules.\n\nIHS EHI Schema Definition\nSeptember 2023\n\nElectronic Health Information Export\n\n1\n\n\fRPMS Electronic Health Information (EHI) Export (BREH)\n\nVersion 1.0\n\nThe DRS, as defined in the HIPAA Rules (45 CFR 164.501), consists of the\nfollowing:\n•\n\nMedical records and billing records about individuals.\n\n•\n\nEnrollment, payment, claims adjudication, and case or medical management\nrecord systems maintained by or for a health plan.\n\n•\n\nOther records that are used, in whole or in part to make decisions about\nindividuals.\nNote: The term record means any item, collection, or grouping of\ninformation that includes protected health information.\n\nAn EHI export does not include the following:\n•\n\nPsychotherapy notes as defined in 45CFR 164.501.\n\n•\n\nInformation compiled in reasonable anticipation of, or for use in, a civil, criminal,\nor administrative action or proceeding.\n\nIHS EHI Schema Definition\nSeptember 2023\n\nElectronic Health Information Export\n\n2\n\n\fRPMS Electronic Health Information No additional EHI export documents were found beyond the 4 files in the `EHI_Schemas` folder. The PDF itself references only the IHS IT support contact page.\n\n## What Was Found\n\n### Overview\n\nIHS has built a dedicated EHI export package called **BREH (RPMS Electronic Health Information Export)** that generates patient-level exports in JSON format using a predefined national schema. This is a genuine (b)(10) implementation — not a repackaged FHIR API.\n\n### Documentation Set\n\n1. **IHS_EHI_Schema_Definition.pdf** (241 KB, 7 pages, September 2023): A brief user manual explaining the BREH package. Key points:\n   - The BREH package allows authorized users to manually generate an EHI export for a single patient or a patient population\n   - Exports are generated using a **predefined national schema** published on the IHS website (the `.txt` files)\n   - Sites can also create **custom schemas** based on the national schema\n   - Export format is **JSON**\n   - \"All EHI information is available in an EHI export, with the exception of images. Imaging links/data are presented but not the images themselves\"\n   - The PDF correctly defines EHI per 45 CFR 171.102 and excludes psychotherapy notes and litigation compilations\n\n2. **BREH_OIT_20230607.txt** (5.6 MB, June 2023): Initial national schema — 296 patient files, ~8,221 field definitions\n3. **BREH_OIT_20240703.txt** (5.7 MB, July 2024): Updated schema — 298 patient files, ~8,274 field definitions\n4. **BREH_OIT_20250714.txt** (5.8 MB, July 2025): Latest schema — 301 patient files, ~8,415 field definitions, 548 pointer/reference files\n\n### Schema Structure\n\nEach schema file is a single JSON object with the following top-level keys:\n- `SCHEMA NAME`: Version identifier (e.g., \"BREH_OIT_20250714\")\n- `VERSION STATUS`: \"ORIGINAL VERSION\"\n- `PUBLIC URL`: Points back to the IHS FTP page\n- `EXPORT TYPE`: \"EHI\" (also supports \"4DW PAMPI\" and \"4DW ALL\" for data warehouse exports)\n- `PATIENT`: Array containing a single template entry with `DFN` (patient identifier) and `FILE` (301 file/table definitions)\n- `POINTER FILE`: Array of 548 reference/lookup tables\n\nEach patient file definition includes:\n- File metadata (global, EHI-enabled flag, custom file flag, file version)\n- Field definitions with: field type, FileMan data dictionary info (file-subfile number, global reference, node, piece), constraints (min/max length, bounds, decimal places), pointer references to other files, value set codes\n\n### Field Type Distribution (2025 schema)\n\n| Field Type | Count |\n|-----------|-------|\n| POINTER TO A FILE | 2,291 |\n| FREE TEXT | 1,445 |\n| SET OF CODES | 1,411 |\n| DATE/TIME | 1,251 |\n| NUMERIC | 698 |\n| COMPUTED | 573 |\n| WORD PROCESSING | 554 |\n| WORD-PROCESSING | 170 |\n| VARIABLE-POINTER | 21 |\n| MULTIPLE COMPUTED | 1 |\n| **Total** | **8,415** |\n\n### Schema Evolution\n\nThe schema has grown steadily across versions:\n- **2023 → 2024**: Added 2 files (BJVN HL7 EXCEPTIONS, CHS CHEF REGISTRY), field count changes in 13 files\n- **2024 → 2025**: Added 3 files (BLRAU ANTIMICROBIAL USE LOG, BPHR MED REFILL REQUEST, V DELIVERY), field count changes in 12 files (notably PATIENT went from 171→194 fields, MHSS RECORD from 125→143 fields)\n\nNo files have been removed across any version.\n\n## Export Coverage Assessment\n\n### Data Domain Coverage\n\nIHS RPMS's EHI export schema is **remarkably comprehensive** and represents one of the most thorough (b)(10) implementations in the industry. The 301 files cover the full breadth of RPMS's clinical and administrative data:\n\n**Clinical Data — Thoroughly Covered:**\n- **PCC V-files (43 files)**: The complete Patient Care Component visit-linked data — measurements, medications, procedures, diagnoses (POV), providers, immunizations, lab results, microbiology, pathology, radiology, dental, eye glass, health factors, skin tests, hospitalizations, emergency visits, elder care, well-child, nutrition screening, telehealth, patient education, stroke, AMI, anticoagulation, asthma, and more\n- **Clinical notes (4 files)**: TIU DOCUMENT (all clinical notes), TIU EXTERNAL DATA LINK, TIU MULTIPLE SIGNATURE, TIU PROBLEM LINK\n- **Pharmacy (13 files)**: PRESCRIPTION, PHARMACY PATIENT, PHARMACY ARCHIVE, controlled substance audit, DEA archive, interventions, Surescripts requests, drug accountability, medication verification\n- **Laboratory (7 files)**: LAB DATA, LAB ORDER ENTRY, BLRA LAB AUDIT, BLS LOINC EXPORT, antimicrobial use, blood inventory, patient lab data\n- **Behavioral health (27 files)**: Complete MHSS system — intake, records, case dates, groups, treatment plans, treatment notes, problems, goals, methods, suicide forms, personal history, health factors, measurements, prevention activities, CPT codes, providers, Navajo referral forms, CD staging tool\n- **Dental (4 files)**: Dental patient, dental procedure, deferred services register, followup\n- **Immunizations (7 files)**: BI PATIENT system — patient records, contraindications, forecast errors, immunizations due, deleted immunizations, IZ exports\n- **Imaging (6 files)**: IMAGE, IMAGE AUDIT, IMAGING ANNOTATION, MULTI IMAGE PRINT, PACS MESSAGE, TELEREADER (note: actual image files are not included per PDF documentation, but metadata and links are)\n- **Allergies**: PATIENT ALLERGIES (file 120.8), ADVERSE REACTION ASSESSMENT, ADVERSE REACTION REPORTING\n- **Problem list**: PROBLEM file (9000011)\n- **Orders**: ORDER file (100), NON-VERIFIED ORDERS, OE/RR PATIENT EVENT\n- **Consultations/Referrals**: REQUEST/CONSULTATION, REFERRAL PATIENT, RCIS system (6 files)\n- **Care plans**: CARE PLAN, TREATMENT PLAN, PATIENT GOALS\n- **Specialty data**: Reproductive factors, birth measurement, prenatal problems, podiatry history, elder care, women's health (BW system), diabetes management (BCDM), HIV management (HMS REGISTRY), community health (CHR system), chronic disease (CIC VISIT), workman's compensation\n\n**Administrative & Financial Data — Thoroughly Covered:**\n- **Billing (35 files)**: 3P BILL, 3P CLAIM DATA, A/R system (7 files), BILL/CLAIMS, BILLING PATIENT, CLAIMS TRACKING, INTEGRATED BILLING ACTION, INSURANCE CLAIMS, CDMIS billing\n- **Insurance eligibility**: MEDICAID ELIGIBLE/CLAIMS, MEDICARE ELIGIBLE/CLAIMS, PRIVATE INSURANCE ELIGIBLE/CLAIMS, RAILROAD ELIGIBLE/CLAIMS, ABSP COMBINED INSURANCE, ABSP ELIGIBILITY, AGEV INSURANCE ELIGIBILITY\n- **Registration (40 files)**: PATIENT (9000001) with 194 fields, VA PATIENT (file 2) with 436 fields, enrollment data, demographic data, name changes, legal documents\n- **Scheduling (10 files)**: Appointments, scheduled visits, wait lists, SD wait list, transfer requests\n\n**Other Patient-Level Data:**\n- Advance directives, notice of privacy practices, restricted health information, patient refusals for service, family history, personal history, guarantor information, beneficiary travel, spenddown information, prior authorizations, patient implanted devices\n\n### What's Not in the Schema\n\nComparing against the product research, the schema covers virtually all patient-level clinical and billing data. Notable observations:\n\n- **Images are excluded by design**: The PDF explicitly states \"Imaging links/data are presented but not the images themselves in accordance with certification requirements.\" Image metadata files (IMAGE, IMAGING ANNOTATION) are included.\n- **No quality reporting aggregates**: GPRA/clinical reporting data (BGP system) is not in the schema — this is appropriate as those are aggregate quality metrics, not individual patient records.\n- **No audit logs beyond clinical**: The schema includes BLRA LAB AUDIT and IMAGE AUDIT (clinical audit trails) but not general system access audit logs — this is appropriate as system audit logs are not part of the designated record set.\n- **No provider credentialing or system configuration**: Appropriate exclusions.\n- **MailMan/messaging not included**: Internal system messaging is not in the schema. This is borderline — if messages contain clinical content about patients, they could be EHI, but they are primarily an operational communication system.\n\n### Export Format & Standards\n\nThe export format is **native JSON** reflecting the RPMS FileMan database structure. This is:\n- **Not FHIR**: The export uses RPMS's own data model, not HL7 FHIR. Fields reference FileMan file numbers, globals, nodes, and pieces.\n- **Not a standardized format**: The JSON structure mirrors the MUMPS/FileMan database schema directly. Field names are the FileMan data dictionary field names.\n- **Comprehensive but requires RPMS knowledge to interpret**: A receiving system would need to understand the FileMan data model to parse the export. The pointer references (2,291 pointer fields across 548 reference files) create a complex relational structure.\n- **Self-documenting**: The schema files themselves serve as a data dictionary — each field definition includes type, constraints, validation rules, and pointer targets. The export data would follow this schema structure.\n\nThis is an appropriate format for (b)(10). The requirement doesn't mandate any standard — a comprehensive database dump in JSON with a published schema is a legitimate and thorough approach. The fact that it's in the native database format means nothing is lost in translation to a different standard.\n\n### Documentation Quality\n\n**Strengths:**\n- The schema files are **machine-readable JSON** — excellent for automated processing\n- Field definitions include **data types, constraints, value sets, and cross-references** — this is more detail than many commercial vendors provide\n- Three years of versioned schemas show **active maintenance** (2023, 2024, 2025)\n- The schema is **self-contained** — the 548 pointer files provide the lookup tables needed to interpret coded references\n- The PDF correctly scopes EHI per 45 CFR 171.102\n\n**Weaknesses:**\n- The PDF is **very brief** (4 pages of content in 7 pages). It describes what the BREH package does but provides no examples, no sample export output, no walkthrough of the JSON structure.\n- There are **no worked examples or sample export files**. A developer trying to build an importer would need to reverse-engineer the JSON structure from the schema definition files alone.\n- **No user guide** for how to actually perform an export from the RPMS EHR GUI — only a statement that it \"allows authorized users to manually generate an EHI export.\"\n- **No documentation of the JSON output format** — the schema defines what data is included and its field types, but doesn't show what the actual export JSON looks like (key names, nesting structure, array handling).\n- Field definitions lack **human-readable descriptions** — field names are generally descriptive (e.g., \"BLOOD TYPE\", \"BIRTH CERTIFICATE NO.\") but there are no narrative descriptions explaining what each field contains or how it should be interpreted.\n\n### Structure & Completeness\n\n- **Granularity**: Excellent. 8,415 field-level definitions across 301 files, with data types, FileMan metadata, pointer references, and value sets for coded fields.\n- **Relationships**: Documented via POINTER TO A FILE fields (2,291 of them) and 548 pointer/reference file definitions. The relational structure is complete and machine-readable.\n- **Value sets**: 1,411 SET OF CODES fields include their full value lists in the schema.\n- **Versioning**: Three annual versions (2023, 2024, 2025) with clear version names and dates. The schema has grown from 296 to 301 files and from ~8,221 to 8,415 fields.\n- **Change history**: The schema diff shows that changes are additive (new files, new fields) with no removals — suggesting careful backward compatibility.\n\n## Access Summary\n- Final URL (after redirects): https://www.ihs.gov/rpms/applications/ftp/?parent=&fld=EHI_Schemas\n- Status: found\n- Required browser: no (direct curl download works)\n- Navigation complexity: direct_link (all files listed on a single page with download links)\n- Anti-bot issues: none (User-Agent header recommended but standard browser UA suffices)\n\n## Obstacles & Dead Ends\n\nNone. The documentation was straightforward to access. No additional EHI export documents were found beyond the 4 files in the `EHI_Schemas` folder. The PDF itself references only the IHS IT support contact page.\n\n## What Was Found\n\n### Overview\n\nIHS has built a dedicated EHI export package called **BREH (RPMS Electronic Health Information Export)** that generates patient-level exports in JSON format using a predefined national schema. This is a genuine (b)(10) implementation — not a repackaged FHIR API.\n\n### Documentation Set\n\n1. **IHS_EHI_Schema_Definition.pdf** (241 KB, 7 pages, September 2023): A brief user manual explaining the BREH package. Key points:\n   - The BREH package allows authorized users to manually generate an EHI export for a single patient or a patient population\n   - Exports are generated using a **predefined national schema** published on the IHS website (the `.txt` files)\n   - Sites can also create **custom schemas** based on the national schema\n   - Export format is **JSON**\n   - \"All EHI information is available in an EHI export, with the exception of images. Imaging links/data are presented but not the images themselves\"\n   - The PDF correctly defines EHI per 45 CFR 171.102 and excludes psychotherapy notes and litigation compilations\n\n2. **BREH_OIT_20230607.txt** (5.6 MB, June 2023): Initial national schema — 296 patient files, ~8,221 field definitions\n3. **BREH_OIT_20240703.txt** (5.7 MB, July 2024): Updated schema — 298 patient files, ~8,274 field definitions\n4. **BREH_OIT_20250714.txt** (5.8 MB, July 2025): Latest schema — 301 patient files, ~8,415 field definitions, 548 pointer/reference files\n\n### Schema Structure\n\nEach schema file is a single JSON object with the following top-level keys:\n- `SCHEMA NAME`: Version identifier (e.g., \"BREH_OIT_20250714\")\n- `VERSION STATUS`: \"ORIGINAL VERSION\"\n- `PUBLIC URL`: Points back to the IHS FTP page\n- `EXPORT TYPE`: \"EHI\" (also supports \"4DW PAMPI\" and \"4DW ALL\" for data warehouse exports)\n- `PATIENT`: Array containing a single template entry with `DFN` (patient identifier) and `FILE` (301 file/table definitions)\n- `POINTER FILE`: Array of 548 reference/lookup tables\n\nEach patient file definition includes:\n- File metadata (global, EHI-enabled flag, custom file flag, file version)\n- Field definitions with: field type, FileMan data dictionary info (file-subfile number, global reference, node, piece), constraints (min/max length, bounds, decimal places), pointer references to other files, value set codes\n\n### Field Type Distribution (2025 schema)\n\n| Field Type | Count |\n|-----------|-------|\n| POINTER TO A FILE | 2,291 |\n| FREE TEXT | 1,445 |\n| SET OF CODES | 1,411 |\n| DATE/TIME | 1,251 |\n| NUMERIC | 698 |\n| COMPUTED | 573 |\n| WORD PROCESSING | 554 |\n| WORD-PROCESSING | 170 |\n| VARIABLE-POINTER | 21 |\n| MULTIPLE COMPUTED | 1 |\n| **Total** | **8,415** |\n\n### Schema Evolution\n\nThe schema has grown steadily across versions:\n- **2023 → 2024**: Added 2 files (BJVN HL7 EXCEPTIONS, CHS CHEF REGISTRY), field count changes in 13 files\n- **2024 → 2025**: Added 3 files (BLRAU ANTIMICROBIAL USE LOG, BPHR MED REFILL REQUEST, V DELIVERY), field count changes in 12 files (notably PATIENT went from 171→194 fields, MHSS RECORD from 125→143 fields)\n\nNo files have been removed across any version.\n\n## Export Coverage Assessment\n\n### Data Domain Coverage\n\nIHS RPMS's EHI export schema is **remarkably comprehensive** and represents one of the most thorough (b)(10) implementations in the industry. The 301 files cover the full breadth of RPMS's clinical and administrative data:\n\n**Clinical Data — Thoroughly Covered:**\n- **PCC V-files (43 files)**: The complete Patient Care Component visit-linked data — measurements, medications, procedures, diagnoses (POV), providers, immunizations, lab results, microbiology, pathology, radiology, dental, eye glass, health factors, skin tests, hospitalizations, emergency visits, elder care, well-child, nutrition screening, telehealth, patient education, stroke, AMI, anticoagulation, asthma, and more\n- **Clinical notes (4 files)**: TIU DOCUMENT (all clinical notes), TIU EXTERNAL DATA LINK, TIU MULTIPLE SIGNATURE, TIU PROBLEM LINK\n- **Pharmacy (13 files)**: PRESCRIPTION, PHARMACY PATIENT, PHARMACY ARCHIVE, controlled substance audit, DEA archive, interventions, Surescripts requests, drug accountability, medication verification\n- **Laboratory (7 files)**: LAB DATA, LAB ORDER ENTRY, BLRA LAB AUDIT, BLS LOINC EXPORT, antimicrobial use, blood inventory, patient lab data\n- **Behavioral health (27 files)**: Complete MHSS system — intake, records, case dates, groups, treatment plans, treatment notes, problems, goals, methods, suicide forms, personal history, health factors, measurements, prevention activities, CPT codes, providers, Navajo referral forms, CD staging tool\n- **Dental (4 files)**: Dental patient, dental procedure, deferred services register, followup\n- **Immunizations (7 files)**: BI PATIENT system — patient records, contraindications, forecast errors, immunizations due, deleted immunizations, IZ exports\n- **Imaging (6 files)**: IMAGE, IMAGE AUDIT, IMAGING ANNOTATION, MULTI IMAGE PRINT, PACS MESSAGE, TELEREADER (note: actual image files are not included per PDF documentation, but metadata and links are)\n- **Allergies**: PATIENT ALLERGIES (file 120.8), ADVERSE REACTION ASSESSMENT, ADVERSE REACTION REPORTING\n- **Problem list**: PROBLEM file (9000011)\n- **Orders**: ORDER file (100), NON-VERIFIED ORDERS, OE/RR PATIENT EVENT\n- **Consultations/Referrals**: REQUEST/CONSULTATION, REFERRAL PATIENT, RCIS system (6 files)\n- **Care plans**: CARE PLAN, TREATMENT PLAN, PATIENT GOALS\n- **Specialty data**: Reproductive factors, birth measurement, prenatal problems, podiatry history, elder care, women's health (BW system), diabetes management (BCDM), HIV management (HMS REGISTRY), community health (CHR system), chronic disease (CIC VISIT), workman's compensation\n\n**Administrative & Financial Data — Thoroughly Covered:**\n- **Billing (35 files)**: 3P BILL, 3P CLAIM DATA, A/R system (7 files), BILL/CLAIMS, BILLING PATIENT, CLAIMS TRACKING, INTEGRATED BILLING ACTION, INSURANCE CLAIMS, CDMIS billing\n- **Insurance eligibility**: MEDICAID ELIGIBLE/CLAIMS, MEDICARE ELIGIBLE/CLAIMS, PRIVATE INSURANCE ELIGIBLE/CLAIMS, RAILROAD ELIGIBLE/CLAIMS, ABSP COMBINED INSURANCE, ABSP ELIGIBILITY, AGEV INSURANCE ELIGIBILITY\n- **Registration (40 files)**: PATIENT (9000001) with 194 fields, VA PATIENT (file 2) with 436 fields, enrollment data, demographic data, name changes, legal documents\n- **Scheduling (10 files)**: Appointments, scheduled visits, wait lists, SD wait list, transfer requests\n\n**Other Patient-Level Data:**\n- Advance directives, notice of privacy practices, restricted health information, patient refusals for service, family history, personal history, guarantor information, beneficiary travel, spenddown information, prior authorizations, patient implanted devices\n\n### What's Not in the Schema\n\nComparing against the product research, the schema covers virtually all patient-level clinical and billing data. Notable observations:\n\n- **Images are excluded by design**: The PDF explicitly states \"Imaging links/data are presented but not the images themselves in accordance with certification requirements.\" Image metadata files (IMAGE, IMAGING ANNOTATION) are included.\n- **No quality reporting aggregates**: GPRA/clinical reporting data (BGP system) is not in the schema — this is appropriate as those are aggregate quality metrics, not individual patient records.\n- **No audit logs beyond clinical**: The schema includes BLRA LAB AUDIT and IMAGE AUDIT (clinical audit trails) but not general system access audit logs — this is appropriate as system audit logs are not part of the designated record set.\n- **No provider credentialing or system configuration**: Appropriate exclusions.\n- **MailMan/messaging not included**: Internal system messaging is not in the schema. This is borderline — if messages contain clinical content about patients, they could be EHI, but they are primarily an operational communication system.\n\n### Export Format & Standards\n\nThe export format is **native JSON** reflecting the RPMS FileMan database structure. This is:\n- **Not FHIR**: The export uses RPMS's own data model, not HL7 FHIR. Fields reference FileMan file numbers, globals, nodes, and pieces.\n- **Not a standardized format**: The JSON structure mirrors the MUMPS/FileMan database schema directly. Field names are the FileMan data dictionary field names.\n- **Comprehensive but requires RPMS knowledge to interpret**: A receiving system would need to understand the FileMan data model to parse the export. The pointer references (2,291 pointer fields across 548 reference files) create a complex relational structure.\n- **Self-documenting**: The schema files themselves serve as a data dictionary — each field definition includes type, constraints, validation rules, and pointer targets. The export data would follow this schema structure.\n\nThis is an appropriate format for (b)(10). The requirement doesn't mandate any standard — a comprehensive database dump in JSON with a published schema is a legitimate and thorough approach. The fact that it's in the native database format means nothing is lost in translation to a different standard.\n\n### Documentation Quality\n\n**Strengths:**\n- The schema files are **machine-readable JSON** — excellent for automated processing\n- Field definitions include **data types, constraints, value sets, and cross-references** — this is more detail than many commercial vendors provide\n- Three years of versioned schemas show **active maintenance** (2023, 2024, 2025)\n- The schema is **self-contained** — the 548 pointer files provide the lookup tables needed to interpret coded references\n- The PDF correctly scopes EHI per 45 CFR 171.102\n\n**Weaknesses:**\n- The PDF is **very brief** (4 pages of content in 7 pages). It describes what the BREH package does but provides no examples, no sample export output, no walkthrough of the JSON structure.\n- There are **no worked examples or sample export files**. A developer trying to build an importer would need to reverse-engineer the JSON structure from the schema definition files alone.\n- **No user guide** for how to actually perform an export from the RPMS EHR GUI — only a statement that it \"allows authorized users to manually generate an EHI export.\"\n- **No documentation of the JSON output format** — the schema defines what data is included and its field types, but doesn't show what the actual export JSON looks like (key names, nesting structure, array handling).\n- Field definitions lack **human-readable descriptions** — field names are generally descriptive (e.g., \"BLOOD TYPE\", \"BIRTH CERTIFICATE NO.\") but there are no narrative descriptions explaining what each field contains or how it should be interpreted.\n\n### Structure & Completeness\n\n- **Granularity**: Excellent. 8,415 field-level definitions across 301 files, with data types, FileMan metadata, pointer references, and value sets for coded fields.\n- **Relationships**: Documented via POINTER TO A FILE fields (2,291 of them) and 548 pointer/reference file definitions. The relational structure is complete and machine-readable.\n- **Value sets**: 1,411 SET OF CODES fields include their full value lists in the schema.\n- **Versioning**: Three annual versions (2023, 2024, 2025) with clear version names and dates. The schema has grown from 296 to 301 files and from ~8,221 to 8,415 fields.\n- **Change history**: The schema diff shows that changes are additive (new files, new fields) with no removals — suggesting careful backward compatibility.\n\n## Access Summary\n- Final URL (after redirects): https://www.ihs.gov/rpms/applications/ftp/?parent=&fld=EHI_Schemas\n- Status: found\n- Required browser: no (direct curl download works)\n- Navigation complexity: direct_link (all files listed on a single page with download links)\n- Anti-bot issues: none (User-Agent header recommended but standard browser UA suffices)\n\n## Obstacles & Dead Ends\n\nNone. The documentation was straightforward to access. The IHS FTP file browser page loaded correctly, all 4 files were publicly downloadable without authentication, and the JSON schema files were well-formed and parseable.\n\nThe page does include a login/registration panel, but the file downloads work without authentication. The login may be required for other folders on the FTP site (e.g., RPMS software packages) but not for the EHI_Schemas folder.\n","structuredPatch":[],"originalFile":null}}
